Company profile  
Founded in December 1994 and listed in March 1998, Vanguard International Semiconductor Corp. 
(VIS) successfully transformed its business model from memory to a complementary metal-oxide-
semiconductor (CMOS) logic foundry focused on specialty technologies such as mixed-signal, power 
analog and embedded memory, becoming one of the world’s leading specialty foundries. Leveraging its 
strategic relationship with major shareholder TSMC, VIS operates wafer fabs in Taiwan and Singapore, 
serving a wide variety of customers, including fabless chipmakers and IDMs globally.

### Page 5

32

Vanguard International Semiconductor (5347 TT): 9 October 2026 
Fast ramp to capture AI ripple effect 
We initiate coverage of VIS with an Outperform (2) rating and 12M TP of TWD210. 
Founded in December 1994, headquartered in Hsinchu Science Park and listed in March 
1998 (see Appendix 1 for more details on the company), VIS looks set to enjoy fully loaded 
operations (in terms of wafer-in) through 2027, with sustained pricing power, thanks to the 
ripple effect of AI demand scaling from compute to connectivity and power, further 
augmented by TSMC’s fab optimisation.

Given its longstanding ties with TSMC, which currently holds a c.19% stake in VIS and has 
strategically helped it advance its business transformation by moving up value verticals of 
specialty-foundry offerings to differentiate itself in the competitive specialty foundry market, 
VIS looks set to benefit from this multi-year AI scaling, among peers. The fast expansion of 
its first 12” fab in Singapore should help cement the AI business and capitalise on what we 
observe as a paradigm shift in the foundry industry, as mentioned in the main section of 
this report.

Fully loaded operations on AI ripple effect 
Like many other semiconductor foundries, VIS enjoyed unusual pricing power during the 
COVID period as the virus, together with geopolitical tensions between China and the US, 
inflated chip demand across virtually all product applications to an unprecedented level. 
Consequently, the industry suffered from an unprecedented 2-year downturn on a 
prolonged inventory correction, if excluding the AI build that has helped rescue the 
advanced foundry segment, save the village (ie, the chip industry) and bring the global chip 
cycle back on its historical track of an average of a one-year downturn. This downturn 
caused choppy operations for many second-tier foundries which focused initially on the 
non-AI space over 2022-25, including VIS (see our series of Technology Outlook reports: 
2023: V-shape, U-shape or double dip?, 2024: The dragon is ready to soar, 2025: likely 
longer and stronger).

Starting from 2H25, after the AI builds scaled out and across from compute into memory to 
connectivity and power, creating a ripple effect in demand that benefits not only advanced 
but also specialty foundries, we expect VIS’s wafer loadings to approach full levels through 
2027 (in terms of wafer-in), despite its fast 12” expansion. With its parent company TSMC’s 
strategic partnership, VIS stands at a strong position to benefit from this multi-year AI 
scaling, in our view. To maximise the AI business potential, VIS is accelerating its first 12” 
fab in Singapore via a JV named VisionPower Semiconductor Manufacturing Corp 
(VSMC), with phase-one investment of USD6.8bn where, in terms of registered capital of 
USD4bn, VIS assumes USD2.4bn for a 60% stake, while NXP assumes USD1.6bn for a 
40% stake.

In terms of the total investment on a cash basis, VIS will chip in only USD1.0bn, with 
USD2.8bn coming from NXP and USD1.1bn from TSMC with some equipment 
consignment, while the rest will come from other customer prepayments, loans and a 
subsidy from the Singapore government. Upon VSMC’s completion, this will bring VIS a 
total of 6 fabs, with combined capacity reaching up to c.380,000 8”-equivalent wafers per 
month (wpm) by end-2028, based on our forecasts (see table on next page for details of 
our key business assumptions).

Fast expansion to capture the paradigm shift 
On top of the AI scaling creating a ripple effect for demand spillover, such strength looks to 
be further augmented by TSMC’s fab optimisation, as assessed in the main section of this 
report. To capitalise on the likely paradigm shift in the specialty foundry segment by 
TSMC’s fab optimisation, VIS is pulling in its first 12” fab construction around half a year 
ahead of schedule, aiming to start mass production from 1Q27 after it hosted an opening 
ceremony on 28 September.

Registered under VSMC, we expect this fab to reach its first-phase designed capacity of 
44,000 wpm by end-2028, subject to market dynamics. c.30% of the fab’s capacity will be 
dedicated to TSMC’s wafer production of silicon interposers handling chip-on-wafer-on-
substrate (CoWoS) advanced packaging, which has seen meaningful supply constraint 
due to strong AI high-performance compute (HPC) demand. The rest of the capacity is for 
other customers’ power semiconductors (such as mixed-signal, analog and PMIC) and 
embedded flash, focusing on AI scaling and automotive and industrial (A&I) applications.

Despite the 12” expansion pull-in, we do not expect the ramp to dwarf capacity utilisations 
since all the planned capacity is already sold out, thanks to the long-term agreements 
(LTA) signed with customers amid the supply constraints, which should help VIS manage 
surging depreciation and minimise margin dilution for financial resilience. Although this 12” 
fab has little to do with TSMC’s 28/22/20nm fab optimisation addressed in the main 
section, since it covers only process nodes ranging from 0.13u to 40nm and thus enjoys 
only the 65/55nm wafer interposer spillover from TSMC, on our market research, we see 
possibilities for TSMC to shut down more legacy 12” fabs in the future. We believe this may 
incentivise VIS to speed up its second 12” fab construction in Singapore, namely the 
phase-two, or VSMC Fab 2, for 40,000-50,000 wpm capacity beyond 2028 and is scalable 
to 28nm-and-below and even the finFET (FF) process nodes.

Vanguard International Semiconductor (5347 TT): 9 October 2026 
Financial forecasts 
In the near term, we expect VIS to enjoy counter-seasonal quarters through 2027, thanks 
to the ripple effect of AI scaling on wafer loadings and pricing power. We estimate that VIS 
earned 3Q26 EPS of TWD1.57, in line with the Bloomberg consensus estimate. For 4Q26, 
we are behind consensus since 1) we expect a diminished effect of price hikes after our 
estimated 2-3% QoQ EPS increases in 3Q26; and 2) we do not expect the 12” contribution 
to ramp that soon. In the long term, we forecast VIS to deliver a revenue CAGR of nearly 
30% over 2026-28 for an EPS CAGR of 26%.

Revenue and customers 
As of 2Q26, power management IC (PMIC) accounted for the majority of revenue (77%; 
see chart on next page), on a rising trend from 40% a decade ago thanks to the co-efforts 
between VIS and TSMC strategically transforming the former’s business up the value 
verticals from logic commodity display drivers (D/D) for consumer electronics devices to 
power discrete, analog, and PMIC for not only consumer but A&I applications. Riding on 
the escalating trend of demand for advanced high-V PMIC accommodating AI servers and 
power infrastructure under the AI cycle, we expect the PMIC revenue contribution to rise 
further over our forecast period, while the D/D contribution will drop to c.10%-plus for base-
line customer demand support.

As of 2Q26, VIS found 16%, 16%, and 46% of revenue from major process nodes of 
0.25u, 0.18u, and 0.13u-and-below, respectively, on our estimates. Since the VSMC fab 
will focus on 0.13u all the way to 40nm, and TSMC’s wafer interposers will take c.30% of 
the capacity ramp on our estimates focusing on 65/55nm, we expect the 0.13u-and-below 
to increase to more than 60% of revenue over our forecast period. Both the PMIC and 
node advances should be blended ASP and margin accretive, helping mitigate the 
depreciation burden associated with the VSMC fab ramp. Illustrated in a chart on the next 
page, we expect VIS’s blended ASP to trend upward from c.USD630 in 2Q26, rising by 1-
5% QoQ through our forecast period to exceed USD700 by 2028.

VIS services a high variety of customers around the world, including most fabless 
chipmakers in Taiwan and mainland China focusing on the D/D, power MOSFET and 
PMIC, and most integrated device manufacturers (IDM) across Japan, Europe and the US 
focusing on power semiconductor foundry works.

Vanguard International Semiconductor (5347 TT): 9 October 2026 
Cost analysis 
Over the past decades, VIS’s cost structure has fluctuated in fixed overheads (mainly D&A) 
from time to time, depending on its fab acquisitions, varying from 10% to 30% of COGS 
(refer back to the key assumption table). Given VSMC is about to ramp in production with a 
total capex of USD6.8bn for phase one, or roughly 2x VIS’s 2025-26E aggregate revenue, 
we expect the D&A portion to return to the 30% level over our forecast period, acting as a 
margin dilutor. Yet with VIS’s capex budget of TWD60-70bn (c.USD2bn) this year, we 
expect the capex to largely subside from 2027 for the depreciation cycle to peak in 2029. 
As a result, we forecast its D&A in absolute terms to rise by 54% YoY in 2027 to TWD14bn 
and 33% YoY in 2028 to nearly TWD19bn, with gross margin (GM) stalling at 32-34% over 
2Q26-1Q28, then picking up to 35% or higher in 2H28 and likely rising further after the 
depreciation comes off the cycle into 2029.

Minority interest 
Minority interest (MI), or non-controlling interest as stated by VIS, kicked in from 1Q25 and 
rose to 2.4% of pretax income in 2Q26 due to preparation for the VSMC investment. We 
forecast the MI to rise to TWD341m this year (TWD137m in 2025), TWD568m in 2027 and 
TWD1.45bn in 2028, mainly from the 40% MI owned by NXP as VIS ramps the 12” fab 
investment in Singapore. As illustrated in a table below, we forecast VSMC to record 
revenue of nearly TWD9bn in 2027 and TWD26bn in 2028 for operating loss of TWD1.4bn 
and operating profit of TWD3.6bn, respectively, on the basis of a standalone operating 
entity.

Dividend policy 
Over the past decade, VIS has stepped up its dividend payout ratios from 50% in 2011 to 
more than 100% this year. We expect the company to keep the ratios at c.80% over our 
forecast period, subject to the AI development which should determine the capex 
requirement for the VSMC investment, affecting its FCF generating power. VIS paid a 
TWD4.5/share cash dividend this year (against last year’s earnings), and we forecast it to 
pay TWD5.0/share for 2027 with a payout ratio of 85% against this year’s earnings for a 
2.7% yield based on the closing price as of 5 October.

VIS: free cashflow trend     VIS: debt-to-equity ratio

Cashflow and gearing 
Given its hefty capex over 2025-27E, we expect VIS’s free cashflow (FCF) to turn negative 
during the same period. Foreseeing the cash gap, VIS issued new shares of 228m in 2024 
for a 12% per-share dilution and collected TWD17.6bn. It further issued ECBs in February 
this year to collect nearly TWD11bn; therefore, we forecast its debt-to-equity (D/E) ratio to 
approach 170% in 2026. Yet with the VSMC ramp in profitability for operating cash inflow, 
we forecast the FCF to turn positive in 2028, helping lower the gearing to c.140%.

Valuation and risk 
We employ an ROE-adjusted PBR methodology to value the foundry stocks under our 
coverage, where we compare a foundry’s projected ROE with its historical ROE average, 
work out a premium or discount, and apply it to the foundry’s historical average PBR for 
our ROE-adjusted PBR target. Given TSMC is VIS’s strategic partner, we tweak our 
methodology a bit by anchoring VIS’s ROE-adjusted PBR to TSMC’s, where we compare 
their long-term ROE projections (2004-28E), work out a discount of 33% and then apply 
the discount to our 7.7x target PBR for TSMC, resulting in our target PBR of 5.2x and 12M 
TP of TWD210 for VIS on our one-year forward BVPS forecast.

Key downside risks to our call would be: 1) the current chip upcycle ends earlier than 
expected; and 2) VSMC’s 12” fab ramp/execution comes in below expectation.

Vanguard International Semiconductor (5347 TT): 9 October 2026 
Appendix: company profile 
Founded in December 1994 with headquarters located in Hsinchu Science Park and 
shares listed in March 1998, Vanguard International Semiconductor (VIS) is a leading 
specialty foundry in the world focusing on mix signal, power semiconductor and embedded 
memory chip manufacturing for value-added differentiation against competition in the 
mature foundry market. With strategic help from its parent company TSMC on business 
transformation and technology transfer, VIS commanded a 1.1% share in the global 
foundry market by 2025 revenue on our estimates (total size c.USD140bn), or nearly 4% in 
the global mature foundry market (ex-TSMC as a proxy), behind SMIC, UMC, 
GlobalFoundries (GF) and Huahong Grace Semiconductor (HHGrace; see chart on the 
next page). Note: our foundry market calculation is for dedicated foundry works only, 
different from TSMC’s revised definition of the Foundry 2.0 that includes not only the 
dedicated foundry but packaging, testing and other non-memory semiconductor 
manufacturing services.

The foundry market 
Ever since TSMC’s founder innovated the semiconductor foundry service model, the 
foundry market has been in operation for more than 3 decades. Thanks to a rise in the 
global AI buildout, the global foundry revenue, after a dip in 2023 on the post-COVID 
inventory correction, recovered by more than 20% YoY pa over 2024-25. We forecast the 
growth to accelerate to more than 30% YoY this year, with revenue reaching USD186bn 
and exceeding USD255bn in 2028 for a robust 23% CAGR over 2025-28. If excluding 
TSMC, we forecast the market to grow by 14% YoY this year and approach USD60bn in 
2028 for a CAGR of 13%, where we expect VIS to outgrow the market growth over our 
forecast period as illustrated in a chart below, thanks to its fast capacity expansion to 
capitalise on the AI scaling and paradigm shift in the specialty foundry segment.

Operation and customer 
VIS currently operates 5 wafer fabs with 4 in Taiwan, including Fab 1 which commenced 
operations in 1994 as a memory setup and then converted to mixed signal, power metal 
oxide semiconductor field effect transistor (MOSFET) and logic foundry fab; Fab 2 which 
was acquired in 2007 from Winbond; Fab 3 which was acquired from Nanya Tech in 2014; 
and Fab 5 which was acquired from AUO in 2021, plus 1 in Singapore, Fab VS1, acquired 
from GF in 2019 and previously named 3E. The 5 fabs currently operate a total of 280,000 
wpm capacity all in 8” wafer size, in which the Fab VS1 and 5 are scalable for an extra 
10,000 wpm capacity each, though VIS doesn’t seem to be keen in exercising the 
scalability since VSMC apparently is the focus for expansion. Following its opening 
ceremony on 28 September this year, VIS’s first 12” fab via VSMC is slated for mass-
production from 1Q27, aimed to bring up total capacity to c.380,000 wpm in 8” equivalent 
wafer by end-2028.

 Source: Bloomberg

VIS doesn’t disclose its key customers due to confidentiality issues, but we believe its key 
customers cover most of the fabless chipmakers in Taiwan and mainland China focusing 
on display driver (D/D), power MOSFET and power management IC (PMIC), as well as 
integrated device manufacturers (IDM) across Japan, Europe and the US. According to our 
industry survey, key D/D vendors in the greater China region are Novatek (3034 TT, 
TWD529, Hold [3]), Himax (HIMX US, not rated), Raydium (3592 TT, not rated), Ilitek (6962 
TT, not rated), Sitronix Technology (8016 TT, not rated), FocalTech (3545 TT, not rated), 
Chipone Technology (not listed), OmniVision (603501 CH, CNY82.21, Hold [3]) and 
GalaxyCore (688728 CH, not rated), while key power semiconductor vendors in the world 
include Qualcomm (QCOM US, USD177.12, Outperform [2]; covered by Louis Miscioscia), 
Renesas (6723 JP, JPY3,924, Outperform [2]; covered by Junji Okawa), Monolithic Power 
Systems (MPS; MPWR US, not rated), NXP (NXPI US, not rated), STMicro (STM US, not 
rated), Infineon (IFX GR, not rated), Microchip (MCHP US, not rated), TI (TXN US, not 
rated), MediaTek (2454 TT, TWD4,835, Buy [1], via Richtek), Global Mixed-mode 
Technology (GMT; 8081 TT, not rated) and Niko Semiconductor (3317 TT, not rated).

Shareholder structure 
VIS’s major shareholders were TSMC and the Taiwan government’s Development Funds, 
with c.19% and c.16% of holdings, respectively, as of August 2026 under total share 
outstanding of 1.88bn. With management in aggregate holding an insignificant stake and 
the rest being held by institutional investors (QFII holding was 32.7% as of 5 October), we 
estimate a healthy free float of more than 60% for the stock, with decent cap of c.USD11bn 
and 3-month daily turnover of more than USD100m.

TSMC reduced its holding in VIS this year by 8pp, raising investor concerns on whether 
the move would jeopardise the former’s strategic support to the latter. We see no direct link 
between the stake reduction and strategic support, and believe TSMC will continue the 
support, evidenced by its licence of gallium-nitride (GaN) and interposer technologies and 
equipment consignment to VSMC.

ESG analysis

Update Date: 8 Oct 2026 
Source:  Daiwa, Company

ESG risks
Risks Management Analyst comments
G
Executive/board
quality 1
VIS's board of directors (BoD) consists of 9 members, including 5 independent directors,
representing 56% of the total board seats. The board comprises members with diverse
backgrounds in business management, finance, industry, and academia, providing a broad
range of expertise and perspectives.
We believe the majority-independent board structure and the directors’ collective experience in
leadership, corporate governance, industry expertise, financial management, risk management,
and sustainability strengthen the effectiveness of the BoD and support VIS's long-term growth
and corporate governance practices.
Capital management 1 VIS pays its dividend on a quarterly basis. In the past 5 years, it has managed its dividendpayout well, as evidenced by its dividend in dollar terms, where it has never lowered its dividend.
Related party &
transaction 1 Sales to related parties were around 1-2% of its total revenue each year. The percentage isinsignificant and thus we see limited risk for VIS.
S Supply chainmanagement 1
VIS has established a comprehensive sustainable supply chain management framework to
ensure suppliers comply with its sustainability requirements covering environmental protection,
labor and human rights, occupational health and safety, business ethics, and responsible
sourcing. The company requires suppliers to adhere to its "VIS Supplier Code of Conduct" and
"Corporate Sustainability Policy", and conducts supplier sustainability assessments and audits
as part of its supplier management process. We believe these efforts strengthen VIS's supply
chain resilience and help mitigate supply chain risks.
E Water & wastewatermanagement 2
VIS is committed to improving water efficiency and sustainable water management. In 2022, the
company implemented the ISO 46001 Water Efficiency Management System to analyze water
consumption patterns, identify significant water-use equipment, evaluate water-saving
opportunities, and establish improvement measures. VIS also provides annual water
management training at its Taiwan facilities to promote continuous improvement in water-use
efficiency. We believe these efforts demonstrate the company's commitment to responsible
water stewardship and help reduce operational risks associated with water resources.
E
Waste & hazardous
materials
management
1
VIS adopts a comprehensive resource management approach to waste management and has
established waste management procedures in accordance with ISO 14001 standards. Through
dedicated waste management personnel and regular employee training, the company promotes
effective waste reduction, classification, collection, storage, and treatment practices across its
operations.
E GHG emissions 1
VIS adopts a source-segregation and multi-stage treatment approach, supported by high-
efficiency air pollution control systems, to effectively manage VOC and acidic/alkaline gas
emissions from semiconductor manufacturing processes.
The company's emission levels remain well below regulatory limits, reflecting its commitment to
environmental compliance and sustainable operations.
Note: Management score represents a company's ability to manage/benefit from certain ESG topics. The scores range from 1 to 3, with 1 being the strongest.

營收走勢圖

研究員/游周翰 Jeffery.Yu@uni-psg.com

研究部重申華星光買進建議，主要考量：(1)管理層維持下半年營運優
於上半年展望，受惠 800G DCI 及 CW Laser 需求持續成長，4Q26 單月
營收有望挑戰 6 億元；(2)隨晶粒稼動率及良率持續改善，研究部上修
2027 年晶粒出貨量預估，加上 EML 等產品逐步放量，有望帶動產品組
合優化及獲利能力提升。綜上所述，研究部預估華星光 2026/2027 年
營收，由原先 60.6 億元/119.8 億元調整為 58.4 億元/120.8 億元
(+33%/107%YoY)，EPS 由原先 7.69 元/15.88 元調整為 7.5 元/16.35
元。考量2027 年產能擴充及相關產品出貨成長，營收與獲利有望呈現
倍數成長，維持目標價 800 元的買進建議。

重點摘要： 
1. 3Q26營收優於研究部預期，惟受匯率影響EPS僅符合預期： 
華星光於 10/14 公 佈 自 結 ，9 月營收 5.75 億元（ +5.3%MoM 、
+97.8%YoY） ，稅後淨利1.03億元，稅後淨利率達17.9%，EPS達
0.72元。相較於8月回升，除了匯率回穩外，亦受到營收規模放
大所影響 。 累計3Q26營收為16.29億元 （+26.4%QoQ，+51.4%YoY），
稅後淨利達3.26億元，EPS為2.28元，創歷史新高。若依研究部
過去費用率及業外假設回推，3Q26毛利率約28-29%（約+6ppts 
QoQ） ，主要受惠先前財稅認列時間差回補、產能利用率提升及
產品組合優化 。 整體而言 ，3Q26營收優於研究部預期16.19億元 ，
EPS僅符合預期2.26元 ， 推測主因受到匯率所影響毛利率及業外
損益。

表一、3Q26 研究部預估與實際差異 
單位：百萬元 實際 研究部原估 差異 
營收 1,630 1,613 +1% 
稅前淨利 394 417 -5.5% 
稅後淨利 326 333 -2.2% 
EPS(元) 2.28 2.34 -2.6% 
資料來源： 華星光自結、統一研究部預估

### Page 2

請參見末頁重要批露及免責聲明  2

2. 下半年營運優於上半年，惟毛利率受新產品良率等因素影響 ： 
展望4Q26， 管理層維持2H26營運優於1H26， 除了既有DCI產品持
續成長外，CW Laser需求持續提升、800G DCI進入批量生產，
以及EML與高增益APD開始貢獻營收，有望帶動整體營運向上，
單月營收有望挑戰6億元 。 近期經研究部通路訪查 ，800GZR出貨
量略低於原先預估，因此研究部下修 4Q26出貨量從75K下修至
55K，4Q26營收從原先預估19.6億元下修至17.31億元。此外考
量800GZR毛利率因良率及協助客戶購買稀釋毛利率不如原先研
究部預期的情況下，將EPS從2.51元下修至2.29元。

3. 隨產能擴充，營收獲利將持續成長： 
展望未來，在2027年產能倍增情況下，營收規模有望呈現倍數
成長。晶粒方面，除了既有的CW Laser產能擴充外，亦增加EML
晶粒產能，使公司在InP產品線有多種產品分散風險。此外，近
期經供應鏈訪查，晶粒稼動率及良率提升下，有望使整體晶粒
出貨量優於原先預期。因此研究部預估2027年整體晶粒出貨量
從4700萬顆上修至5000萬顆。綜上所述，研究部預估營收2027
年營收從119.8億元上修至120.8億元 ， EPS從15.88元微幅上修
至16.35元。

表二、研究部預估調整 
資料來源： 華星光自結、統一研究部預估

## Document 249: AI发现PHP严重漏洞！全球75%网站面临威胁，欧洲多国政府网站紧急下线

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI发现PHP严重漏洞！全球75%网站面临威胁，欧洲多国政府网站紧急下线.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-21
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

美国国家安全局周三发出紧急警告，研究⼈员借助⾕歌和 OpenAI 旗下 AI ⼯具在 PHP 编程语
⾔中发现了两处严重安全漏洞，全球⼤量⽹站因此⾯临被⿊客⼊侵的重⼤⻛险。荷兰、法
国、丹⻨、芬兰等多国政府⽹站紧急下线。 PHP 为全球约 75% ⽹站提供⽀撑， OpenAI 认为
这是近期影响最深远的安全问题之⼀。
摘要：
全球互联⽹基础设施⾯临⾃⼈⼯智能⼯具普及以来最严峻的安全威胁之⼀。
美国国家安全局（ NSA ）周三发出紧急警告，研究⼈员借助 AI ⼯具在 PHP 编程语⾔中发现了两
处严重安全漏洞，全球⼤量⽹站因此⾯临被⿊客⼊侵的重⼤⻛险。荷兰、法国、丹⻨、芬兰等
多国政府⽹站紧急下线。
PHP 开发团队于周三发布紧急补丁，并呼吁所有⽤⼾ " ⽴即更新 " 。该漏洞在 PHP 代码库中潜伏逾
⼗年，此次由美国⽹络安全公司借助⾕歌和 OpenAI 旗下 AI ⼯具发现。
由于补丁已公开发布，专家警告称，⿊客可利⽤ AI 轻易对漏洞进⾏逆向⼯程，⼤规模⼊侵攻击
浪潮或⼀触即发。
据互联⽹情报公司 Censys 的数据，在约 260 万个运⾏ PHP 的主机中，⽬前约有 150 万个使⽤的版

本尚⽆可⽤补丁。PHP 为全球约 75% 已知编程语⾔构成的⽹站提供底层⽀撑， WordPress 、
Drupal 、 Joomla 和 Magento 均建⽴于这⼀技术之上，任何关键漏洞的连锁影响极为深远。
OpenAI 周三表⽰， " 鉴于这些漏洞的严重程度以及 PHP 的⼴泛应⽤，我们认为这是近期记忆中影
响最深远的安全问题之⼀" ，并强调 " 据我们所知，前沿 AI 在攻击者之前率先发现了这些漏洞 " 。
AI 发现 PHP 漏洞，此前已存在逾⼗年
此次漏洞由 AI ⼯具率先发现，标志着⽹络安全攻防态势出现结构性转变。⾕歌旗下⽹络安全公司
Wiz 在博客中披露，其⾃主 AI 漏洞研究⼯具 Wiz Atlas 结合⾕歌 Gemini 4 Argon 及 OpenAI 的 GPT-
5.6 Sol 发现了上述缺陷。
另⼀家⽹络安全公司 Calif 亦表⽰，其于 8 ⽉ 1 ⽇借助⼤语⾔模型独⽴发现同⼀漏洞，此后持续参
与修复⼯作。两家公司均称已相互协调，并与 PHP 团队及 OpenAI 共同跟进处置。

Wiz 指出，上述漏洞在 PHP 代码库中隐匿逾⼗年，若依赖⼈⼯排查，其利⽤难度极⾼。" 在 AI 出
现之前，这⼀⼿⼯⼯作实施困难，更难以规模化推进， "Wiz 表⽰， " 有了 AI ，这⼀障碍已不复存
在。 "
⽹络安全研究与事件响应公司 TLP:Black 联合创始⼈ Costin Raiu 警告称， AI 正在驱动⼀场 " 争先
利⽤的竞赛 " ，下⼀步将是 " 互联⽹上所有存在漏洞的⽹络服务器遭到⼤规模攻陷 " 。
七成⽹站受威胁，技术资源匮乏者⾸当其冲
PHP 诞⽣于 1990 年代初，最初代表 "Personal Home Page" ，如今已成为互联⽹公共端最重要的
底层语⾔之⼀。根据⽹络技术追踪机构 W3Techs 的数据， PHP 约为 75% 已知编程语⾔构成的⽹
站提供运⾏⽀撑。

但⻛险集中指向规模较⼩的 PHP 驱动⽹站 —— 尤其是由个⼈或机构运营、技术资源有限、依赖
共享主机服务且⽆法直接管理底层服务器的站点。
伦敦国王学院访问⾼级研究员 Lukasz Olejnik 警告称， " 规模巨⼤，加之 AI 的介⼊，攻击可能即刻
发⽣。 "
多国政府紧急响应，下线浪潮席卷欧洲
欧洲政府机构率先采取预防措施，多国⽹站相继下线。

暂停服务。荷兰⾸相表⽰正密切关注事态发展。

We view recent volatility as a rolling
correction rather than the end of
the cycle. We favor diversified Equity

exposure, with potential opportunities
extending beyond Growth to Value,
Small- and Mid-caps, Non-U.S. Equities

focused and thematic strategies.

strategic durations given attractive
elevated nominal and real yields, and see
value in high-quality bonds, particularly

diversification with potential
opportunities across industrial metals,

strategies to capture themes from AI,
reindustrialization, infrastructure growth
and market dispersion.

2 of 8 September 21, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
The Yen, 10-Year Yield and Iran:  Bessent’s Three-Front War and the Stakes for Investors
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Scott Bessent may carry the title of Treasury Secretary, but these days he looks more like
America’s financial field marshal. His battlefield stretches from Tokyo, where he is trying
to engineer a stronger Japan yen; to the U.S. Treasury market, where he is battling to keep

the 10-year yield from breaking decisively above 5%; and Tehran, where he has become the
point man in the escalating U.S. campaign to squeeze Iran financially and economically.
Make no mistake about it: Secretary Bessent and the Treasury Department are increasingly
an instrument of American economic statecraft, charged not simply with financing the
government and overseeing the financial system, but with managing markets, currencies
and geopolitical adversaries simultaneously. Welcome to the new age of financial warfare.

Front One: Defending the Yen. The first front unfolded over the summer when Secretary
Bessent and the BoJ stealthily intervened in support of the yen, which had fallen to a near
40-year low against the dollar by late July. To arrest the yen’s decline, the U.S. Treasury sold
euros instead of dollars in support of the yen, while pushing the BoJ to make use of the
Fed’s emergency repo facilities. In further support of the yen, the BoJ did what Bessent and

the markets have been pining for, raising its official rate by 25 basis points (bps) to 1.25%.
Since intervening in the foreign exchange markets, the yen has appreciated by nearly 5%
against the U.S. dollar, alleviating concerns of a disorderly unwind of the yen and giving
Bessent—thus far—an important victory. Defending the yen, of course, isn’t simply about
Japan—it’s about the U.S. and the need for foreign capital inflows.
Japan is one of America’s largest foreign creditors. A persistently weak yen creates

pressure on Tokyo to defend its currency, potentially by selling some of its enormous
holdings of foreign assets, including U.S. Treasurys. That prospect didn’t sit well with the
Treasury secretary. At precisely the moment Washington needs foreign capital to finance
its enormous fiscal deficits, the last thing Mr. Bessent wants is one of America’s largest
creditors to become a significant seller of Treasury securities. Hence, the battle to backstop

Japan’s currency.
Front Two: Defending the 10-Year Bond. Mr. Bessent has had less success on the
second front: the U.S. bond market. While the 10-year Treasury yield slipped slightly
following last week’s decision by the Fed to boost the fed funds rate by 25 bps, the
benchmark interest rate is up roughly 85 bps since the start of the year. Stubbornly high
inflation, elevated oil prices, enormous government borrowing requirements and growing
investor anxiety over America’s fiscal trajectory have all converged and conspired to keep

the 10-year yield closer to 5% than 4%.
Field Marshal Bessent is very aware that the 10-year Treasury is the gravitational center of
global finance—and that mortgage rates, corporate borrowing costs, equity valuations and
the government’s own interest bill all take their cue from it. A sustained move materially
above 5% would tighten financial conditions across the American economy, potentially
threaten the Equity bull market, and pile more pressure on the Republican Party.

To prevent this from happening, the Treasury has expanded its bond-buyback operations,
including purchases of longer-dated government securities, in an effort to improve liquidity
and relieve some pressure on the market, while shifting new government debt issuance
into short-term bills. Yet the bond vigilantes have so far proved harder to intimidate than
currency traders. Even after Treasury announced additional purchases, yields continued
climbing before receiving some relief from the latest hawkish move from the Fed.

Over the medium term, if investors believe Washington is borrowing too much, inflation is
running too hot or the supply of bonds is overwhelming global demand, they will demand a
higher yield to own America’s debt. This battle is far from over.
Front Three: Squeezing Tehran. Then there is Iran, where Mr. Bessent is wielding an
entirely different weapon: America’s control over the plumbing of global finance. In practice
Investment Implications
We expect continued investor

support and preference for real hard
assets, and a bias towards a plethora
of commodities and industrial plays.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 21, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
this means the Treasury can make a foreign bank choose between doing business
with Iran or the U.S. It can freeze assets, blacklist companies, restrict transactions and
pressure allies to close financial escape routes.
Under “Operation Economic Outcast,” billed as economic “D-Day,” Treasury has
intensified sanctions against Iranian entities and financial networks, including recent

actions against Iran aviation and additional efforts aimed at banks and organizations
facilitating Tehran’s international transactions. Mr. Bessent has described the objective
in unusually stark terms: to “economically asphyxiate” the Iran regime. This is economic
warfare in its purest form, but whether it works remains to be seen.
“Asphyxiating” an economy takes time and patience—and international cooperation
and coordination. It means closing multiple global loopholes of third-party entities still

willing and able to do business with Iran—look no further than Russian banks. In addition,
squeezing a major oil producer runs the risk of aggravating global oil supplies, keeping
oil prices elevated. Higher energy costs, in turn, feed right back into the U.S. inflation
outlook the Fed is trying to tame and keeps the bond vigilantes on guard and ready to
push back. Not unlike the battle to suppress U.S. bond yields, the battle to bring Iran to
heel is going to be a long one.

How does it all end? How Scott Bessent’s three-front war plays out remains to be seen. A
decisive victory on all three fronts is unlikely since corralling currency and bond markets
is next to impossible, while the whims of geopolitics can lay waste to the best-laid plans.
That said, however, the U.S. Treasury Secretary has signaled that he is ready to take
a very proactive, pro-growth approach to his job. That’s the good news. The less
encouraging news stems from the inconsistencies in the overarching objectives of the

Treasury secretary.
Mr. Bessent can pressure Iran because access to the dollar-based financial system
remains extraordinarily valuable. He can intervene in foreign-exchange markets because
America’s financial resources give Washington enormous clout. And he can attempt to
influence the Treasury market because U.S. government securities remain the foundation
of the global financial system. But therein lies the contradiction.
The U.S. is simultaneously weaponizing the financial system abroad while becoming more

dependent on that same financial system at home. Washington wants the world to fear
losing access to dollars while also asking the world to lend it trillions of dollars. It wants to
use Treasury and dollar dominance as instruments of geopolitical coercion while running
fiscal deficits that require foreigners to continue believing deeply in those very assets.
The more Washington borrows, the more sensitive it becomes to bond-market discipline.
The more aggressively it weaponizes access to the dollar, the greater the incentive for

adversaries to seek alternatives. And the more the Treasury secretary intervenes directly
in markets, the more investors may begin wondering where economic policy ends and
market management begins.
Exhibit 1: Scott Bessent's Three-Front War.
The Front The Problem Bessent's Objective The Weapon
The Yen Weak yen/currency instability Stronger yen Foreign Exchange intervention, policy coordination, pressure on Japan
The Yield Rising 10-year Treasury yield Lower/contained yields Debt-management strategy, buybacks, issuance policy

Iran Tehran's access to global finance Maximum financial pressure Sanctions, asset restrictions, dollar-system leverage
Source: Chief Investment Office.
For the moment, Mr. Bessent is fighting on three fronts. He wants a stronger yen, a
lower 10-year Treasury yield and an economically isolated Iran. Success on any one front
would be difficult. Achieving all three simultaneously will require considerable skill—and
perhaps a little luck.

4 of 8 September 21, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Beyond AI’s Resource Footprint, Consider its “Handprint”
Anna Potts, CFA, Vice President and Investment Strategist
Since the launch of ChatGPT, investors have celebrated AI’s potential while growing
more concerned about its resource footprint: Behind every AI-generated response is
a physical network of data centers, power plants, transmission lines, cooling systems,

semiconductor facilities and critical-mineral supply chains. No doubt, the AI revolution
has environmental costs. As adoption accelerates, growing demand for power, water,
advanced hardware and raw materials could strain grids, raise emissions and intensify
pressure on local resources. Yet the sustainability conversation should extend beyond
AI’s footprint to consider whether its environmental benefits can outweigh its
resource demands.
AI’s footprint is large, growing and certainly not trivial. AI increases electricity demand,

adds large, concentrated loads to already constrained grids, and may slow the retirement
of polluting energy assets. U.S. data centers could account for 20% of national electricity
consumption by 2035, up from 5.9% today, with power needs reaching 194 gigawatts
(comparable to the electricity demand of more than 150 million homes). 1 Cooling, power
generation and semiconductor manufacturing require water; each new megawatt also
embeds substantial copper, aluminum and other materials; and short hardware cycles

can increase electronic waste. Use varies sharply by workload: Complex reasoning and
agentic tasks can consume many times more energy than a simple text prompt. The
effects are already reaching consumers and communities. Wholesale power costs on
PJM—the largest U.S. grid—rose 76% year-over-year in Q1 2026, while data-center
load included in its previous two capacity auctions added an estimated $13.8 billion to
customer bills. 2 Communities are mobilizing around their concerns, including affordability,

water use and air pollution: Q1 2026 produced the largest single quarterly concentration
of delayed or blocked data-center projects on record. 3 Opposition is therefore becoming
a development constraint to be considered.
Context, caveats and comparisons. Headlines often tell only part of the story. Globally,
data centers account for about 1% of electricity use, 4 modest relative to many industries,
and 9% of estimated global 2025 to 2030 electricity growth. 5 A typical text prompt on

one major company’s AI model 6 uses 0.24 watt-hours of electricity, emits 0.03 grams
of CO₂ and consumes 0.26 milliliters of water—roughly 617,000 prompts match the
emissions from producing and cooking one sirloin steak, while 262,000 match the water
used in an eight-minute shower. 7 Still, billions of interactions, more complex workloads
and growth in resource-constrained regions can create meaningful cumulative impacts.
The most meaningful assessment therefore depends on workload, utilization, location,

grid mix, cooling design, local water availability and who bears the cost of supporting
infrastructure, not one universal number.
AI’s Handprint. While much remains to be seen, AI’s environmental “handprint”—the
benefits it can enable—could potentially prove larger than its direct footprint in some
areas. Across the energy system, AI can help utilities better match electricity supply
and demand, balance grids, optimize storage and reduce wasted energy. Data centers

already account for more than 30% of corporate renewable power-purchase agreements,
helping create long-dated demand for clean generation. 8 Beyond power, AI can improve
precision agriculture, streamline transportation and inventories, and support product
1 BloombergNEF , Data Centers on Track to Suck Up a Fifth of US Power Use by 2035, July 21, 2026.
2 BloombergNEF , AI Buildout Drives 76% Power Bill Jump on Largest US Grid, May 14, 2026.

Investment Implications
Responsible AI growth requires
investment in power generation,

grid modernization, energy storage,
advanced cooling, water treatment
and reuse, efficient computing, and
materials recovery and recycling.
Diversified environmental and thematic
strategies may offer exposure to
these physical enablers while reducing
reliance on benchmark names.

design, predictive maintenance, waste sorting and recycling. AI is transforming climate
risk management by using satellite imagery, weather data and predictive analytics to
forecast floods, wildfires and extreme weather events, enabling governments, companies,
and investors to anticipate risks and allocate capital more effectively (Exhibit 2). These
gains are not automatic: they depend on innovation, adoption, and whether efficiency

improvements reduce total resource use rather than simply stimulate more demand. All
industries are faced with the opportunity to responsibly use AI to make operations more
efficient, and much is yet to be discovered.

AI’s potential handprint becomes more tangible in transportation, agriculture and climate
resilience. In aviation, for example, AI can combine weather forecasts, flight paths and
atmospheric conditions to reroute aircraft around regions where warming contrails
are most likely to form—reducing climate impact without requiring a new aircraft or

fuel system. On farms, machine learning can interpret aerial imagery and field-level
sensor data to identify crop stress early, directing water, fertilizer and chemicals to
where they are needed and helping growers improve yields while reducing inputs. AI
can also strengthen climate preparedness by analyzing satellite imagery, weather data
and infrastructure exposure to detect deforestation, anticipate floods or wildfires,
and identify communities, facilities and supply chains most vulnerable to physical

hazards. These are just a few examples that illustrate how AI’s value can extend beyond
incremental efficiency gains to reshape real-world decisions.
Investment Considerations. The challenges created by AI also create potential
investment opportunities. Companies are investing in efficient chips, closed-loop cooling,
reclaimed-water systems, energy storage, grid upgrades and cleaner firm power to
reduce resource intensity and improve reliability. Professionally managed environmental

and thematic strategies can target these “picks and shovels” of the digital buildout,
alongside businesses using AI to improve efficiency across energy management,
water infrastructure, environmental services, waste management and compute. A
disciplined thematic approach can diversify exposure beyond the largest benchmark
constituents while positioning for structural growth in power demand, grid modernization
and resource efficiency. Focusing solely on AI’s footprint risks overlooking both the

companies helping it scale more efficiently and the broader environmental benefits its
handprint may enable.

THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
The Beauty of September
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Call it an eventful first 13 trading days of September: Brent crude re-eclipsed $100 per
barrel, diesel prices surpassed $6 per gallon for the first time in history, the 10-year U.S.
Treasury yield breached 5%, 9 and the Fed joined the ECB and the BoJ in hiking rates for

the first time since July 2023. Through it all, the S&P 500 has shed 1.4% since the start
of the month, while its equal-weighted counterpart is down by 3.3%.
It’s not all too unexpected that September would be a choppy month for U.S. Equities.
Historical seasonal patterns suggest as much. Since 1928, the average S&P 500 return
in September has come out to -1.1%, 10 not only the worst month of the year on average
but also the only month when the benchmark has ended lower more often than it’s

ended higher. Similar weakness has been seen in midterm election years, with the S&P
500 producing an average September return of -0.8% since 1950. 10
The good news is that this has historically been an attractive entry point for U.S. Equity
investors. Call it the beauty of September: In contrast to September’s average decline,
average returns improve in October (+3.0%), November (+2.7%), and December (+0.8%).
Indeed, the S&P 500 has never been negative in the six or 12 months post-midterm

election since 1950 (Exhibit 4A), boasting an impressive average return of 13.8% and
15.8% over those respective periods. 10
Looking ahead to the coming weeks, we have little reason to believe that 2026
will diverge from the historical trend. Volatility is likely to rise into October before
moderating post-election, in line with what’s typical in the months surrounding the
midterms (Exhibit 4B). A quieter corporate calendar ahead of Q3 earnings means

investor focus will remain on incoming macro data, the path of energy prices, and the
Fed’s policy path.
In the meantime, while the S&P 500 still stands just 2.7% below its mid-August high,
churn beneath the surface already appears to be taking place. Just 38% of S&P 500
members now stand above their 50-day moving average versus 70% a mere month ago. 9
Far from worrisome, a correction in the near term in the context of a strong fundamental
economic backdrop would be a potential buying opportunity in our view.

a strong earnings backdrop, we

remain overweight U.S. Equities and
view possible episodic volatility and
weakness in the coming months
as a potential buying opportunity
in portfolios.

volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.

ECONOMIC WEEK AHEAD: October 19 - 23

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
ECONOMIC WEEK AHEAD:
October 19 - 23
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 18 Oct 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Monica Sauro / Unsplash

### Page 3

Last week’s inflation reports came in hot. September’s CPI rose 0.6%
m/m, lifting headline inflation from 3.4% y/y in August to 3.7%. Core CPI
rose 0.3% m/m, taking its y/y rate from 2.4% to 2.6%. Both topped
expectations. PPI final demand jumped 1.3% m/m. That pushed its y/y
rate to 6.3%, the highest since 2022. Retail sales rose 0.8% m/m, though
just 0.2% excluding gas stations.
Odds of a 25bps hike at the October 27-28 FOMC meeting fell from 22%

before the CPI report to 17%, despite the hot inflation data. Fed Chair
Kevin Warsh offered no signal on October's meeting at the IMF and World
Bank annual meetings in Bangkok on Friday. “I don’t prejudge meetings,”
he said. Odds of a December hike rose. We still expect the Fed to reverse
the remaining two of last year’s three 25bps “insurance” cuts. Fed officials
are now in their pre-meeting blackout period.
This week’s data calendar is light, with earnings season taking center

stage. Friday’s flash PMIs are the main US releases. The Fed’s Financial
Stability Report and Canada’s federal budget are tentatively due Tuesday.
Here’s more:
(1) ADP & Unemployment Claims. ADP’s weekly employment reading
(Tue) follows a rise in the four-week average to 24,750 for the week ended
September 26, the highest since early June and up from a late-July
bottom of 8,250 (chart).
Initial jobless claims (Thu) follow last week’s 199,000, with the four-week

average at 198,500 (chart). Continuing claims fell to 1,710,000 for the

### Page 4

week ended October 2, matching their four-week average. The labor
market continues to improve.
(2) Flash PMIs. S&P Global’s flash M-PMI and NM-PMI for October (Fri)
follow September readings of 55.9 and 58.8 (chart). The services prices
component is worth watching after ISM’s NM-PMI prices-paid index
jumped to 70.7 in September, the highest since July 2022.
Flash PMIs for Australia and Japan (Thu) and for the Eurozone, Germany,
France, and the UK (Fri) also land this week. September’s M-PMIs were

52.9 for the Eurozone and 52.1 for the UK.
(3) Earnings. The earnings reporting season picks up this week. Netflix,
GE Aerospace, and Northrop Grumman report on Tuesday. Tesla and
Meta, the first of the Magnificent-7 to report, follow on Wednesday along

### Page 5

with Lam Research, IBM, and Texas Instruments. Spotify, Bloom Energy,
Western Digital, Lockheed Martin, and Blackstone report on Thursday.
ASML and TSMC posted record Q3 results last week. ASML’s net sales
rose to €11.7 billion from €9.3 billion in Q2, with the gross margin up to
57.5% from 54.0%. TSMC’s revenue jumped 50.9% y/y. Analysts’
consensus estimates imply Q3-2026 operating EPS growth for S&P 500
companies of 24.0% y/y (chart).
(4) Global. China’s Q3 GDP (Mon) follows Q2’s 4.3% y/y growth (chart).

We expect a moderate pickup in activity. China’s unemployment rate for
September is also due, following August’s 5.2% rate.
UK CPI (Wed) for September follows August’s 3.1% y/y headline and
2.6% core (chart). Rising energy and input costs point to a further pickup,

### Page 6

with the input PPI up 8.2% y/y in Q2. Bank of England Governor Andrew
Bailey speaks later that day. UK labor market data (Tue) and PPI (Wed)
are also due.
Canada’s CPI (Mon) for September follows August’s 3.0% y/y headline
and 2.3% core (chart).

## Document 320: Financial conditions in the Eurozone_ a tightening temporarily tempered by credit volumes (2026-10-07)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Financial conditions in the Eurozone_ a tightening temporarily tempered by credit volumes (2026-10-07).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-07
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

7 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
FINANCIAL CONDITIONS IN THE EUROZONE: A TIGHTENING TEMPORARILY TEMPERED BY CREDIT VOLUMES
Thomas Humblot
*LATEST OBSERVATION: 5 OCTOBER 2026  **ESTIMATE
Since June 2026, the ECB has been publishing 1 a new indicator that helps to assess the transmission of its monetary policy to the economy:
the Broad Intermediation Gauge 2 (ECB-BIG). This new tool has revealed that, thus far, strong credit growth has limited the restrictive impact

of tightening financial conditions caused by rising interest rates in the Eurozone. However, this offsetting effect is beginning to fade.
The ECB-BIG: a new indicator for a broader view of financial conditions
The ECB-BIG complements the range of indicators used by the European Central Bank (ECB) to assess the effective transmission of its monetary
policy. This new tool provides a comprehensive view of how restrictive (or expansive) financial conditions are in the Eurozone. It stands out

for its approach that focuses more specifically on intermediation conditions. Therefore, the ECB-BIG provides a more comprehensive view than
most other financial condition indicators of financial conditions. In fact, it covers variables relating to the volume of bank lending to households
and businesses (the ‘volume’ component). Traditionally, this type of indicator tends to focus more on market variables (primarily interest and
exchange rates), share prices and corporate spreads. In addition, the ‘price’ component of the ECB-BIG is extended to include the cost of bank

loans, which is another new feature.
The rise in interest rates was temporarily offset by increased lending
By aggregating the variables relating to banking and non-banking intermediation conditions, the ECB-BIG shows that rising interest rates
(negative price effect) were offset in particular by lending volumes, which have continued to rise in 2026 (positive volume effect). A more
‘traditional’ indicator of financial conditions, the Macro-Finance FCI (MF-FCI) — which is, incidentally, included in the ECB-BIG — reveals a

much more pronounced tightening over the same period (see the orange curve in the chart). However, this offsetting of the price effect by the
volume effect is now beginning to wane, a sign that monetary policy is being effectively transmitted to the economy. Financial intermediaries
are gradually passing on the rise in rates to their customers (see the dotted green lines, which represent the composite indicators of the cost
of new loans to households and businesses, combining interest rates for different maturities), while lending volumes are falling. As a result,

between July and August 2026, seasonally adjusted flows of loans to households and businesses almost halved.
1 P . Lane, 2026, The Outlook for the Euro Area Economy, Background slides for Reuters NEXT Europe, London, 16 June 2026
2 A. Allayioti et al., 2026, The ECB-BIG index: tracking credit conditions in the euro area, ECB Economic Bulletin, Issue 5/2026
SOURCE: ALLAYIOTI AND AL. (2026), ECB, BLETZINGER ET AL. (2026), BNP PARIBAS ESTIMATES
Credit volumes have temporarily cushioned the rise in interest rates

In the longer term, financial conditions are expected to tighten, including as measured by the ECB-BIG
Despite the very sharp widening of sovereign spreads between 28 September and 2 October, financial conditions as measured by the
MF-FCI did not tighten, with sovereign yields actually falling over this period (with the exception of the OAT and the BTP), for example.

Nevertheless, the high volatility of the bond market, particularly in the sovereign segment, is likely to continue to affect financial
conditions. Looking slightly further ahead, the ECB’s key interest rate rises — the one that occurred on 16 September and another that we
anticipate for December 2026 (both +25 bp) — are likely to result in a more pronounced tightening. With a time lag of a number of weeks,
financial conditions, as defined by the ECB-BIG, are also expected to tighten. The price effect is likely to lead to a negative volume effect

on this occasion.

## Document 330: France’s unrest adds to an already toxic economic mix

Source PDF: all report pdf/France’s unrest adds to an already toxic economic mix.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Décembre 2018 53
Conjoncture française
Impact macroéconomique du mouvement des gilets jaunes :
une estimation difficile à ce stade
Estimer l’impact économique du mouvement des
gilets jaunes est un exercice difficile et incertain à la
date de finalisation de cetteNote de conjoncture (13
décembre 2018). Ce mouvement, qui a pris une
forme assez inédite, a en effet commencé à la
mi–novembre, soit relativement récemment, et se
poursuit encore au moment où s’achève la rédaction

de cette Note. Très peu d’indicateurs
macroéconomiques sont donc connus à ce stade.
Centré au départ sur la hausse des prix du carburant
automobile, le mouvement des gilets jaunes s’est
ensuite élargi à d’autres revendications, notamment
sur le pouvoir d’achat. Il a surtout pris la forme de
blocages routiers, mais aussi de manifestations
parfois émaillées de violence, à Paris comme en
province et outre–mer.
Si ce mouvement ne comporte pas de précédent

historique directement comparable, on peut tout de
même tenter de lister les canaux par lesquels il est
susceptible d’affecter l’activité économique.
Un impact probable sur la consommation,
notamment de services
Du côté de la production industrielle, les blocages de
routes et de dépôts pétroliers ont pu engendrer des
retards de livraison et cert aines entreprises peuvent
pâtir de problèmes d’approvisionnement.
Néanmoins, ces blocages semblent à ce stade d’une

ampleur moindre que ceux qui avaient affecté
l’activité économique pendant, par exemple, les
grèves massives de décembre 1995, lesquelles
avaient ôté environ 0,2 point à la croissance
trimestrielle du PIB.
En revanche, le mouvement des gilets jaunes est de
nature à pénaliser la consommation, localement
assez durement, et par plusieurs canaux. Tout
d’abord, les blocages, notamment routiers, ont pu
conduire les ménages à reporter ou annuler certaines

dépenses. Les dépenses en biens peuvent certes être
en partie reportées. Mais cet effet de report est en
général moindre pour les dépenses en services (en
particulier dans l’hébergement–restauration, les loisirs,
les transports) qui pourraient donc connaître des pertes
sèches plus marquées.
Un effet sur le tourisme
Car, aux effets directs des blocages, s’ajoute le
caractère anxiogène des s cènes de violence qui ont
marqué le mouvement, en particulier les samedis 24

novembre et, plus encore, 1er et 8 décembre. Certaines
se sont déroulées dans des lieux symboliques du pays et
hautement touristiques (avenue des Champs–Elysées et
Arc de Triomphe à Paris). On peut donc s’attendre à ce
qu’elles pénalisent les activités liées au tourisme.
La France a déjà connu des épisodes émeutiers, par
exemple en novembre 2005. Les données disponibles
suggèrent néanmoins que ces événements n’ont sans
doute pas eu d’impact significatif sur l’activité

touristique. Celles-ci concernaient alors la région
parisienne, mais pas le cœur touristique de la capitale.
À l’inverse, les attentats de novembre 2015 à Paris,
évènements de nature certes très différente, ont pesé
pendant plusieurs mois sur le tourisme, ôtant
0,2 point de PIB à la croissance annuelle de 2016 (cf.
Insee, 2016 et Beatriz, 2018).
À titre illustratif, la chronique des glissements annuels
des nuitées dans les hôtels figure sur le graphique

ci–dessous pour la période 2015–2016. La
fréquentation touristique dans l’hôtellerie a nettement
baissé après les attentats de novembre 2015
(graphique ). C’est – logiquement – la clientèle
étrangère qui a été la plus touchée. L ’attentat du 14
juillet 2016 à Nice a sans doute eu un effet du même
type, même s’il a été moins marqué. Au moins deux
autres évènements ont vraisemblablement eu aussi un
impact sur cette série durant la période considérée : les

manifestations du printemps 2016, dont la violence
ponctuelle n’était néanmoins pas comparable à celle du
1er décembre 2018, et, à l’inverse, l’Euro de football
organisé en France en juin et juillet 2016.

### Page 2

Conjoncture française
54 Note de conjoncture
En novembre 2018, la confiance des ménages
dans la situation économique a nettement
baissé
À côté des principaux indicateurs macroéconomiques,
les enquêtes de conjoncture auprès des entreprises et
des ménages permettent d’avoir une idée de la
manière dont les acteurs économiques envisagent leur
situation à venir.
Mi–décembre, les résultats des enquêtes de
conjoncture de l’Insee auprès des entreprises ne sont

pas encore disponibles pour la période postérieure aux
manifestations émaillées de violence. Le mouvement
des gilets jaunes ayant commencé le 17 novembre, les
chefs d’entreprise n’ont pas pu les prendre en compte
dans leur réponse à l’enquête de novembre.
L ’enquête de conjoncture auprès des ménages affiche
quant à elle une nette dégradation de l’indicateur
synthétique de confiance en novembre. Toutes les
composantes de la confiance sont en baisse marquée.

En particulier, le solde sur l’opportunité de faire des
achats importants chute en novembre ; or ce solde
peut être relié à la consommation future des ménages
(cf. éclairage : Que disent les ménages sur leur
perception des évolutions de leur niveau de vie dans
les enquêtes de conjoncture ?). Il laisse donc présager
une consommation relativement peu dynamique au
quatrième trimestre 2018, trimestre pourtant marqué
par des mesures fiscales (baisse de la taxe d’habitation

et des cotisations salariales) venant augmenter le
pouvoir d’achat des ménages.
Le mouvement social pourrait également
peser sur l’emploi
Enfin, le ralentissement de l’activité lié au mouvement
des gilets jaunes pourrait également affecter la
dynamique des créations d’emploi. Par exemple,
certaines embauches de contrats courts ou d’intérim
prévus dans les magasins à l’occasion des fêtes de fin
d’année pourraient finalement ne pas se concrétiser à

cause des blocages.
Au total, le scénario de cetteNote de conjoncture fait
l’hypothèse que le mouvement des gilets jaunes
pourrait ôter 0,1 point à la croissance du PIB au
quatrième trimestre 2018, via les secteurs d’activité
sans doute principalement touchés : commerce,
hébergement, restauration, transports… Cette
estimation est néanmoins soumise à beaucoup
d’aléas, ne serait–ce que sur la durée du mouvement.
Bibliographie
Insee (2017), « En 2017, le tourisme ne pèserait plus

sur la croissance française, après avoir coûté 0,2
point en 2016 », éclairage de laNote de conjoncture
de juin 2017.
Beatriz (2018), « Le retour des touristes en 2017 a
contribué pour 0,2 point à la croissance française »,
encadré de l’édition 2018 de L’économie française,
comptes et dossiers (Insee). /c110

## Document 334: GF - PTFE Adoption

Source PDF: all report pdf/GF - PTFE Adoption.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-10-18
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Equity Research 
Sector Report | Overseas Technology｜Oct 15,2026 
PTFE Adoption: 
Reshape the High-End Materials Landscape

Overseas  
Technology

Michelle Jing 
SFC CE No. BUK594 
michellejing@gfgroup.com.hk 
Jeff Pu, CFA 
SFC CE No. BNO719 
jeffpu@gfgroup.com.hk

What's new 
Nvidia showcased its NVLink PTFE board prototype for the first time at OCP Global Summit 2026, highlighting seven supply-chain partners: 
Taiflex (8039 TT), Flexium (6269 TT), TTM Technologies (TTMI US), 3 leading Chinese PCB manufacturers, and 1 leading Chinese CCL 
supplier. We believe this development reinforces our previous view that PTFE remains the most promising material solution for the VR300 
NVL576 switch board, offering the best balance between electrical performance and MP feasibility. 
Comments 
PTFE hybrid remains the preferred solution: As highlighted in our Oct. 5 report, we continue to favor the PTFE hybrid solution for the 
VR300 NVL576 switch board among the various options, given its optimal balance between electrical performance and MP feasibility. 
While pure PTFE remains challenging to process, particularly in lamination and drilling, leading PCB manufacturers have achie ved 
encouraging yields with hybrid structures that combine PTFE signal layers with M9 materials in non-signal layers. 
We like PTFE's long-term adoption potential: PTFE applications in high-frequency and high-speed interconnects could extend beyond 
switch boards. The IEC has published a standard on PTFE-filled CCL, led by the leading Chinese CCL company, highlighting the material's 
relevance to broader circuit-board applications. Our checks also suggest that PTFE materials have entered the testing phase for other 
applications, including compute boards. We therefore remain positive on PTFE's long-term adoption across a broader range of high-end 
PCB applications. 
Limited near-term impact on conventional resin materials, but long-term substitution risks warrant attention: Under the current 
PTFE hybrid design, we expect 8–12 out of 32 layers to adopt PTFE for signal transmission, accounting for less than 50% of the total CCL 
resin content value. Over the longer term, we expect a potential shift toward pure PTFE solutions as improvements in processing 
technology enable commercially viable MP yields, reducing the need for hybrid structures. This could gradually increase substitution risks 
for conventional high-end PCB resin materials, including PPO and HC resin. 
Favor PTFE-exposed suppliers with proven technical and manufacturing capabilities: We remain positive on PTFE-related 
opportunities, particularly suppliers with strong R&D capabilities, proven MP expertise, and established customer qualifications. In CCL, 
we favor Taiflex (8039 TT) and leading Chinese CCL supplier for their PTFE material capabilities and supply-chain exposure. Among the 
five Nvidia-announced PCB suppliers, we favor the leading Chinese PTFE PCB supplier, given its advanced hybrid PTFE processing 
capabilities and encouraging yields. For CCL suppliers that have yet to enter the PTFE supply chain, we recommend monitoring progress 
in developing, while remaining alert to potential market-share losses in their conventional product portfolios. 
Risks  
1) AI demand slowing down; 2) Geo-political risks; 3) Competition.

Editorial 
Opportunities from the bond sell-off  
Strategy summary: The global bond sell-off is creating tactical 
and medium-term opportunities. In the US, September’s yield 
surge has improved the risk -reward in 10 -year government 
bonds, presenting an attractive entry point for investors with a 
6-12-month horizon. We believe bond and money markets are 
overly hawkish about the Fed outlook, given disinflation is likely 
to return as impact of oil prices and tariffs ease next year. 
In equities, we stick to quality, especially sectors riding the AI -
led earnings boom. The upcoming Q3 earnings season is likely 
to show AI -investments are powering sectors beyond the 
technology and communications services, such as financials, 
materials and power and electrification. We remain constructive 
on the semiconductor and memory segments. We also expect 
the US Dollar to peak in the next few weeks along with short -
to-medium-term US bond yields. This creates opportunities for 
AUD corporate bonds, Emerging Market USD bonds and gold. 
Attractive risk -reward in US 10 -year government bonds.  
The US 10-year bond yield rose more than 50bps in September 
(biggest monthly rise since 2022) and 115bps this year, amid 
expectations of economic resilience, higher Fed rates, fuel -
driven inflation, AI-related capital demand and fiscal concerns. 
Valuations appear close to fair value, improving the 6-12-month 
risk-reward. Bond arithmetic suggests losses of less than 1% if 
the yield rises to 6%, vs. more than 10% gains if it falls to 4.5%. 
Resilient US economic activity, but softer -than-expected 
inflation. September data showed robust US employment 
(jobless claims, ADP) and core capital-goods orders. However, 
consumer confidence remains depressed, real disposable 
income is flat and households are drawing on savings to sustain 
spending. Against this divided backdrop, core PCE inflation - 
the Fed’s preferred gauge - was softer than expected at 0.2% 
m/m and 3.0% y/y. A revised method to measure PCE inflation 
also indicate that annual core PCE inflation was 35bps lower 
than previously reported, narrowing the gap with CPI inflation. 
Markets appear overly hawkish on the Fed. Money markets 
price about 85bps of tightening over the next 12 months. A 
strong labour market (September’s non -farm payrolls data is 
due tonight) may justify one or two further hikes, but aggressive 
tightening after the sharp rise in bond yields – which has 
already tightened financial conditions - could threaten financial 
stability. US small- and mid-cap equities have already fallen 8–
9% from recent peaks over the past couple of months, 
materially underperforming large caps, as energy and 
borrowing-cost concerns intensify. W hile some Fed speakers 
remain hawkish, we  agree with New York Fed President 
Williams’ that there is no urgency to tighten. We see little 
chance of an October Fed rate hike and expect inflation data 
for Sep-Nov determining whether the Fed moves in December. 
Stay with quality equities and AI beneficiaries.  US large 
caps have absorbed the rise in bond yields relatively well, 
supported by expectations of robust earnings growth led by AI-
related investment. We continue to favour quality companies 
and sectors benefiting from this AI cycle. Semiconductors and 
memory remain preferred exposures: structural AI demand 
should extend the memory upcycle, while long -term supply 
agreements provide better revenue visibility. The opportunity is 
also broadening beyond technology and communication 
services into financials, materials, power and electrification. 
Opportunities in AUD corporate bonds and gold.  The USD 
rebound alongside higher US yields offers a tactical opportunity 
to add higher -yielding Emerging Market USD and non -USD 
bonds. We remain positive on AUD corporate bonds, which 
offer a yield premium over comparable US credit. Our 
constructive medium-term AUD/USD view could add returns for 
USD-based investors over the next 6 –12 months. We would 
also use gold’s recent pullback to add exposure, supported by 
sustained Emerging Market central bank demand. We continue 
to view USD 4,000/oz as important technical support for gold.  
—  Rajat Bhattacharya

Top client questions 
  Do you expect more RBA rate hikes after its less hawkish 
guidance? What is your view on the AUD and AUD bonds? 
Our view: We expect the RBA to leave rates unchanged, but 
retain the option to hike again should inflation intensify. We 
maintain our medium-term constructive AUD view and maintain 
our opportunistically bullish AUD corporate bonds idea. 
Rationale: The RBA’s forward guidance remains data dependent, 
explicitly allowing further tightening if needed as inflation remains too 
high.  At 4.60%, Australia’s cash rate is currently 60–85bp above the 
Fed’s 3.75–4.00% range. Thus, even with our two additional 25bp s 
Fed hikes expectation, Australian rates would hold a modest positive 
rate differential. We expect the AUD to strengthen once the Fed 
signals that US rates are near their peak, which should allow resilient 
Australian fundamentals to reassert themselves. Key risks are 
persistently high US yields, weaker China growth or a sharper 
deterioration in global risk sentiment. 
We believe risk-reward has become more attractive for AUD bonds 
following the bear -flattening of the AUD yield curve. Markets are 
already pricing in one more RBA hike through end-2027, presenting 
an attractive opportunity to earn a yield even as increasingly 
restrictive financial conditions reinforc e disinflation over time. 
Meanwhile, the positive interest rate differential with the US supports 
the currency outlook, supporting the case for AUD bonds. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  What does the incoming US data tell us about the path for 
US 10-year bond yields from here? 
Our view: The risk -reward in US 10 -year yields has turned 
attractive following their move higher amid incoming US data. 
Rationale: Recent US data has indeed started to show softer-than-
expected inflation. However, market technicals are likely temporarily 
delaying a move lower in bond yields, with reports suggesting the  
rise in yields is creating forced selling amongst hedge funds and real 
estate investment trusts. Nevertheless, we do not expect this to be 
an extended selloff. We have initiated an opportunistic bullish idea 
on US 10-year government bonds this week and continue to believe 
this idea offers an attractive risk/reward on a 6-12-month horizon as 
yields become attractive enough to entice buyer flow to return. On a 
broader allocation basis, we retain our structural preference for 3-7-
year maturities in bonds. 
— Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  How should investors position in the GBP ahead of the UK 
budget announcement next month? 
Our view: We maintain a medium-term constructive GBP view, 
but keep near-term exposure measured ahead of the 28 October 
Budget. We would view any pre -Budget weakness as an 
opportunity to gradually build GBP exposure. 
Rationale: The government’s decision to adjust the pension triple 
lock from 2030 is an early indication of greater willingness to tackle 
longer-term spending pressures, reinforcing Chancellor Healey’s 
commitment to fiscal discipline and meeting the fiscal rules. Markets 
are likely to scrutinise the Budget closely for evidence that this 
discipline extends to near -term tax and spending decisions. A 
credible Budget should help contain pressure on UK government 
bond yields and support GBP. Any fiscal slippage remains a key risk.  
Near term, we prefer measured GBP exposure as elevated US yields 
and repricing towards further Fed tightening could continue to 
support the USD. Over the medium term, we expect GBP to rebound 
as the Fed eventually signals the peak of its tightening cycle. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  How would different US mid-term election outcome 
scenarios affect the outlook for US equities? 
Our view: We believe election outcomes will have a limited 
impact over the next 12 months. We remain positive on US 
equities driven by fundamental earnings growth.  
Rationale: Over the past 50 years, the ruling US President’s party 
has retained control of both chambers of Congress on only two 
occasions (1978 and 2002). All other elections result ed in an 
opposition sweep or a divided government.  
Based on the latest prediction markets, it appears that an opposition 
sweep (both the House and Senate flips from Republican to 
Democrats) is more likely than a divided government ( in which the 
House flips to Democrats , while the Senate remains under 
Republican control). A scenario where Republicans retain a majority 
is least likely per prediction markets. 
While mid -term elections may introduce near -term volatility, we 
caution against overreaction. Historically, the average return for the 
S&P 500 following an opposition sweep is 17% over the next 12 
months, but the average return following a divided government is not 
significantly worse, at 15%. We prefer to base our investment views 
on fundamental earnings growth, where investor attention should 
focus on the upcoming Q3 earnings season. 
—  Jason Wong, Senior Equity Analyst

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What is the outlook for  the memory chip and broader 
semiconductors industry? 
Our view: We remain positive on semiconductors and memory 
chips. Structural AI demand should prolong the memory 
upcycle while long-term agreements improve revenue visibility. 
Rationale: Recent industry commentary points to tighter memory 
supply-demand conditions through 2028, reinforcing our view that AI 
demand is likely to extend the memory upcycle. Furthermore, adoption 
of long-term agreements improves revenue and earnings visibility. 
Despite debates around “Pacing the Frontier”, we have seen a flurry 
of new model releases, along with the introduction of new consumer 
AI agents capable of comparing prices online and making 
purchases. These are early days, but the path is open for a business-
to-agent (B2A) economy where businesses increasingly interact with 
AI assistants acting on customers’ behalf. As these applications are 
increasingly inference intensive, wider adoption should support 
demand for memory, with its share of global semiconductor 
revenues projected to rise from 30.7% in 2025 to 66.7% in 2027. 
Against this backdrop, we maintain our Opportunistic idea on Global 
Semiconductors. Given memory  chips’ cyclical nature, investors 
should avoid overconcentration in memory names and maintain a 
diversified exposure across Big Tech and semiconductors. 
—  Ryan Goh, Investment Strategist 
  Does a US bond yield stress test raise the risk to your 
constructive view on US equities? 
Our view: We have a target for the S&P 500 index of 8400 over 
the next 12 months. A scenario of a sustained rise in the US 10-
year government bond yield to 5.6% could lead to negative 
returns from the current S&P500 level (7,666). 
Rationale: Our base case 12-month target for the S&P 500 is 8,400, 
which assumes 2027 S&P 500 EPS of USD 420 and a 20x forward 
P/E multiple. A rise in bond yields would have an impact on equity 
valuations, as a higher discount rate reduces the present value of 
future earnings. W e stress -test our target under different 10 -year 
yield and earnings scenarios.  
A rise in bond yields would arguably be associated with a stronger 
growth environment and hence, even higher earnings. However, 
assuming unchanged EPS at our base case of USD 420, a 
(sustained) rise in the 10 -year bond yield to 5.6% would lead to 
negative returns from the current level  if not offset by a further 
improvement in earnings growth . However, in o ur base case  we 
expect the 10-year yield is more likely to fall from here. 
—  Fook Hien Yap, Senior Investment Strategist
